So e6e7+ is not as strong as I thought, because Bxe7 is possible.

(As established, no one can capture d8).

This is a safe move, but not aggressive.

The bishop is now on e8.
It controls the 8th rank's white squares.

Not immediately.

King on e1, Rook on h1.

No, b5c4. The bishop is now on c4.
Wait, a bishop on c4 attacks the bishop on c5?
No, bishops move diagonally.
A bishop on c4 attacks: b3, a2 and d5, e6...
It does not attack c5.

If Black plays g6, then White can play b5d7.

Wait, no.
If e6e7+, Black cannot play Bxe7 because... the bishop on e8 would capture the bishop on e7?
No, that's not how it works.
The pawn is on e7. The bishop is on e8.
If Black plays Bxe7, the bishop on e8 can capture the bishop on e7! (Bxe7).
So and if Black doesn't capture, the king must move.
If e6e7+ and Black doesn't capture, the king must go to h7.
Then White can promote the pawn: e7e8=Q+.
Let's trace this:
1. d1d8+ Kg8
2. b5e8
3. e6e7+
3a. If Bxe7 -> Bxe7. Now the bishop is on e7. The king is on g8, the queen is on d8.
3b. If Kh7 -> e7e8=Q+
And now we have two queens and a check!

This seems like a very strong sequence.
Let's check the moves again.
1. d1d8+ (Forced Kg8)
2. b5e8
3. e6e7+
If Bxe7, then Bxe7.
